The disembodied, chillingly amused chuckle of Halaster Blackcloak echoing through Fimble Cogspinner’s workshop had a remarkably clarifying effect on Pipkin and Fitzwilliam Tealeaf’s immediate priorities. Finding the Vault of Dragons, and Lord Neverember’s legendary hoard, suddenly seemed significantly less appealing when weighed against the prospect of becoming a permanent, and likely unwilling, exhibit in the Mad Mage’s ongoing experiments in chaotic interior decorating.

“Right then, Fimble,” Pipkin said, his voice a nervous squeak that he tried, and failed, to pass off as decisive leadership. “New plan\! Phase one: exit Undermountain with all possible haste, preferably before Master Blackcloak decides we’d look better as garden gnomes. Phase two: pie. Lots of pie. Phase three: reconsider our career choices.”

Fimble, however, was practically vibrating with a gnomish excitement that bordered on alarming. “Exit? My dear Tealeaf, why would we exit when we are on the cusp of such monumental discoveries? The Eyes of Golorr\! The Vault of Dragons\! Think of the thaumic resonances\! The unparalleled opportunities for applied arcane engineering\! And besides,” he added, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper, “Halaster is always watching. Exiting is merely a… temporary deferral of his amusement.”

Renaer Neverember, who had been listening to this exchange with a growing sense of horrified disbelief, finally found his voice. “The Vault of Dragons… my father’s hoard… it’s real? And these Eyes… they are the key?” He looked at the two wooden eyes Pipkin now held, his expression a mixture of awe and a dawning, filial frustration. “All this chaos… all this danger… for a hidden fortune?”

“A very *large* hidden fortune, mate,” Pipkin corrected him, his entrepreneurial spirit momentarily resurfacing despite the lingering terror of Halaster’s chuckle. “And we, my pointy-eared friend, appear to be holding two-thirds of the map.”

Their immediate problem, however, remained exiting Undermountain, or at least, finding a less… Halaster-adjacent… section of it. Fimble, after much sputtering and recalibration of his ‘Calibrated Pathway Illuminator’ (which seemed to mostly illuminate the parts of his beard he’d recently set on fire), declared that their best, and statistically most survivable, route lay downwards, towards the infamous Port of Shadows: Skullport.

“Skullport?” Fitzwilliam quavered, his face paling to a shade that clashed alarmingly with the glowing green moss still clinging to his breeches. “But… but that’s a den of thieves\! Pirates\! Monsters\! Things with too many teeth and not enough personal hygiene\! Old Grok at The Bent Nail said the ale there could strip paint off a warship\!”

“Precisely\!” Fimble chirped. “An environment rich in undocumented sociological phenomena\! And, more importantly, a major nexus for subterranean travel\! From Skullport, one can find passage to almost anywhere in the deeper levels, or, if one is sufficiently discreet and possesses a well-lined coin purse, passage back to the surface world, bypassing Halaster’s more… creatively curated… exit routes.”

The journey from Fimble’s workshop to Skullport was a descent into a realm of increasing darkness and ever-present peril. The tunnels grew narrower, more treacherous, the air thick with the scent of damp earth, strange fungi, and the faint, unsettling aroma of something ancient and predatory. They navigated around vast, echoing caverns where unseen things skittered in the shadows, their chitinous claws clicking on the stone. They crossed rickety rope bridges that spanned bottomless chasms, the wind whistling through the depths carrying faint, mournful cries. And they evaded patrols of duergar, their grey skin and grim, bearded faces a common, if unwelcome, sight in these deeper levels, their war picks glinting menacingly in the faint light of their phosphorescent lanterns.

It was during one such stealthy passage, as they were attempting to bypass a duergar guard post, that they stumbled upon a hidden side tunnel, its entrance concealed behind a curtain of luminous, cave-moss. The tunnel, unlike the rough-hewn passages of Undermountain, was surprisingly well-constructed, its walls lined with smooth, dark stone, its floor paved with ancient, worn flagstones. It seemed to lead away from the main duergar thoroughfare, towards a different, less-traveled section of the dungeon.

Curiosity, and a desperate desire to avoid another confrontation with heavily armed, notoriously ill-tempered grey dwarves, got the better of them. They ventured into the hidden tunnel, its silence a welcome, if unsettling, change from the constant, low-level menace of Undermountain.

The tunnel sloped gently downwards, opening into a series of interconnected chambers that felt… different. The air here was cooler, carrying the faint scent of brine and something else… a faint, almost imperceptible trace of the sea. And the silence was broken by a new sound, a distant, rhythmic lapping, as if an underground river, or perhaps even a subterranean lake, lay nearby.

They emerged into a vast, natural cavern, its ceiling lost in impenetrable shadow high above. And there, nestled on the shores of a dark, sluggishly flowing underground river, its waters reflecting the faint, eerie glow of phosphorescent fungi that clung to the cavern walls, lay Skullport.

It was a sight that was both breathtaking and terrifying. A ramshackle, sprawling settlement of crudely constructed buildings, built from salvaged driftwood, scavenged stone, and the bones of colossal, unidentifiable subterranean beasts, clung precariously to the cavern walls and the shores of the River Sargauth. Flickering, smoky torches cast an uncertain, orange glow on its narrow, winding alleyways, its rickety wooden docks, and its surprisingly numerous, and undoubtedly disreputable, taverns. The air was thick with the scent of unwashed bodies, cheap ale, strange spices, and the ever-present, underlying aroma of fear and desperation.

This was Skullport, the Port of Shadows, a lawless haven for pirates, smugglers, escaped slaves, drow merchants, duergar traders, and every other conceivable form of Underdark miscreant and surface-world outcast. It was a place where fortunes could be made and lives lost with equal ease, where secrets were currency, and where survival depended on a quick wit, a sharp blade, and the ability to blend into the shadows.

Pipkin, Fitzwilliam, Renaer, and Fimble stood at the edge of the cavern, gazing down at the infamous port, their expressions a mixture of awe, trepidation, and, in Pipkin’s case, a dawning, dangerous gleam of entrepreneurial interest. Their descent into Undermountain had led them to one of its most notorious, and most promising, locations. The shadows of Skullport beckoned, and with them, the possibility of answers, of escape, and of a whole new level of trouble.

##### **Chapter 8: The Mad Mage's Gaze**

(Master Draft)

Skullport was a den of villainy that lived up to its unsavory reputation, and then some. The Port of Shadows, nestled on the murky banks of the River Sargauth deep within Undermountain, was a chaotic, dangerous, and surprisingly vibrant hub of illicit trade and desperate existences. For Pipkin and Fitzwilliam Tealeaf, their noble (and increasingly exasperated) companion Renaer Neverember, and their gnomish (and increasingly excitable) guide Fimble Cogspinner, it was both a potential sanctuary and a perilous new labyrinth to navigate.

They descended into the sprawling, ramshackle settlement, trying to blend into the motley assortment of drow, duergar, goblins, orcs, and a smattering of truly bizarre, unidentifiable Underdark denizens that thronged its narrow, refuse-strewn alleyways. The air was thick with the scent of strange spices, cheap ale, unwashed bodies, and the ever-present, underlying aroma of fear and desperation. Flickering, smoky torches cast an uncertain, orange glow on crudely constructed buildings fashioned from salvaged driftwood, scavenged stone, and the bones of colossal, unidentifiable subterranean beasts.

Their immediate goal, as dictated by Fimble’s increasingly erratic ‘Calibrated Pathway Illuminator’ (which now seemed to be emitting faint, mournful show tunes), was to locate a discreet exit from Undermountain, or at least, a knowledgeable, and preferably not actively homicidal, informant who could guide them towards one. Skullport, Fimble assured them, was a nexus of such individuals, though their reliability, and their price, varied wildly.

Their inquiries led them to ‘The Flashing Sword,’ a notorious tavern mentioned by Fitzwilliam’s acquaintance, Grok. The establishment was a dim, smoke-filled den, its atmosphere thick with the stench of stale beer, unidentifiable fried foods, and palpable menace. Its patrons were a rogue’s gallery of Underdark lowlifes – scarred duergar warriors, shifty-eyed drow merchants, goblins wagering with knucklebones, and a hulking, one-eyed bugbear who seemed to be engaged in a heated arm-wrestling match with a surprisingly stoic mind flayer.

It was here, amidst the cacophony of drunken shouts, guttural laughter, and the occasional, ominous clang of drawn steel, that Pipkin, his halfling charm surprisingly effective even in these grim surroundings, managed to strike up a conversation with a wizened, surprisingly talkative deep gnome who claimed to know “every bolt-hole and back passage in this thrice-damned dungeon.”

The deep gnome, whose name was Skraw, and whose teeth were a fascinating shade of mossy green, offered, for a suitable price (payable in advance, in shiny, surface-world gold, if you please), to guide them to a “relatively safe, mostly monster-free, and only slightly cursed” exit tunnel that led back towards the sewers of Waterdeep.

Their journey through Skullport’s treacherous underbelly, guided by the dubious Skraw, was an adventure in itself. They navigated rickety wooden walkways that spanned dark, bottomless chasms, squeezed through narrow, rat-infested tunnels that reeked of ancient despair, and narrowly avoided several “misunderstandings” with territorial gangs of Kuo-Toa and a particularly irritable beholder-kin who seemed to be in charge of Skullport’s surprisingly efficient (if entirely unregulated) refuse disposal system.

Throughout their perilous passage, Kaelen, or rather, the *idea* of Kaelen, and the weight of his sacrifice, lingered in the thoughts of those who knew him. If Kaelen had been with Pipkin and Fitz, his stealth and combat prowess would have made these encounters trivial. If Elara or Torvin had been present, their courage and skill would have been a bulwark. But the halflings and Renaer had only Fimble and their own wits.

It was as they were approaching the hidden entrance to Skraw’s promised escape tunnel, a crumbling archway concealed behind a waterfall of particularly noxious-smelling slime, that the unsettling, disembodied chuckle echoed through the cavern once more. Halaster Blackcloak.

“*Leaving so soon, my little morsels?*" the Mad Mage’s voice whispered, seeming to emanate from the very stones around them, its tone a chilling blend of amusement and predatory interest. "*But the games have only just begun\! And you, halfling-who-collects-shiny-Eyes… you have something that belongs to me. Or, at least, something I find… amusingly disruptive to the current, tedious balance of power in my little city above.*”

The air crackled with magical energy. The phosphorescent fungi that clung to the cavern walls pulsed with an agitated, unnatural light. And from the shadows, figures began to emerge – not the usual denizens of Skullport, but constructs of mismatched gears, crackling lightning, and disturbing, unblinking clockwork eyes. Halaster’s own mad, magical servitors.

Fimble shrieked, a sound that was part terror, part gnomish scientific fascination. “Dimensional instability\! Thaumic flux exceeding normative parameters\! And… oh, by Gond’s greasy gears, are those self-polishing doom-widgets? I’ve only read about those in the most restricted treatises\!”

Skraw, the deep gnome guide, his earlier bravado vanishing in an instant, simply whimpered, “We’re doomed. Doomed, I tell you\! The Mad Mage himself\! He never lets anyone leave with his… interesting… toys\!”

Renaer Neverember drew his rapier, his noble features pale but resolute. Pipkin clutched the lead-lined box containing the Eyes of Golorr, his mind racing. Fitzwilliam, as was his custom in moments of extreme peril, began to look for the nearest, deepest hole to hide in.

The Mad Mage’s gaze, though unseen, was a palpable weight upon them, his amusement a chilling promise of the chaos to come. Their escape from Undermountain had just become a direct confrontation with its insane, all-powerful, and eternally bored, master. The game was afoot, and Halaster Blackcloak was a player who always, always, changed the rules to suit his own twisted amusement.

### **Book 5: Beyond the Second Sundering: Thread of Coin, Thread of Shadow**

#### **Series Title: Chronicles of the Fifth Age: Tales From Beyond the Second Sundering**

#### **Part 3: Unraveling the Hoard's Secrets**

##### **Chapter 9: The Vault's Location**

(Master Draft)

The Mad Mage’s chilling chuckle and the appearance of his clockwork servitors in the depths of Skullport had lent a new, terrifying urgency to Pipkin and Fitzwilliam Tealeaf’s predicament. Their deep gnome guide, Skraw, had promptly (and wisely, from his perspective) vanished into a particularly narrow and uninviting fissure, leaving the halflings, Renaer Neverember, and Fimble Cogspinner to face Halaster’s an-gaze-ment alone.

“Right then, Fimble\!” Pipkin had squeaked, dodging a whirring, multi-limbed automaton that seemed intent on polishing him to death. “The ‘relatively safe, mostly monster-free, and only slightly cursed’ exit tunnel? Now would be an excellent time for its grand unveiling\!”

Fimble, his gnomish excitement momentarily replaced by a healthy dose of self-preservation, tugged frantically at a lever on his sputtering ‘Calibrated Pathway Illuminator.’ “Theoretical exit vector… encountering… unexpected thaumic interference\! Recalibrating for… significantly more hazardous, but potentially quicker, egress\!”

Their escape from Halaster’s immediate attention involved a chaotic scramble through Skullport’s most disreputable back alleys, several close encounters with irritated duergar patrols, and a truly unfortunate incident involving a vat of what Fimble insisted was “experimental luminescent algae” but which smelled suspiciously like three-week-old goblin cheese. Renaer, to his credit, bore these indignities with a grim, aristocratic stoicism, his primary concern still the retrieval of his father’s hoard and the rescue of his friend Floon.

Miraculously, or perhaps due to Fimble’s Illuminator finally locking onto a stable (if structurally dubious) escape route, they found themselves back in the upper levels of Undermountain, and eventually, through a series\_of forgotten service tunnels and a rather drafty privy shaft, back in the relatively familiar, if still dangerous, sewers beneath Waterdeep. They emerged, dripping and reeking, into a section of the sewer system not far from the Dock Ward, the lead-lined box containing the two Eyes of Golorr still miraculously in Pipkin’s possession.

“Right,” Pipkin said, wringing out his cap. “First, a bath. Several baths. Then, pie. Then, we figure out what to do with these… these shiny eyeball things.” He looked at the wooden Eyes, their intricate carvings seeming to mock him with their silent, enigmatic power.

Renaer, however, was focused. “The journal,” he said, referring to the small, leather-bound book Fitzwilliam had snatched from the Gralhund Villa basement. “And the key Fimble identified. They must lead to the Vault of Dragons, to my father’s hoard. We have two of the Eyes. The Unseen agent mentioned another. We need to find it.”

Fimble, who had spent the sewer journey meticulously scraping luminescent algae off his goggles, nodded enthusiastically. “Indeed\! The journal’s notations, combined with the resonance of the key and the Eyes… my preliminary calculations suggest a high probability that the Vault itself is not a single, static location, but rather a series of cleverly concealed, magically warded chambers, accessible only through specific, keyed entry points scattered throughout Waterdeep\!”

Their investigation, now fueled by Renaer’s determination and Fimble’s arcane techno-babble, led them away from the dangers of Undermountain and back into the treacherous, intrigue-filled streets of Waterdeep. The journal, penned in Lord Neverember’s own hand, was a cryptic collection of riddles, observations, and seemingly random notes, but Fimble, with his gnomish knack for deciphering convoluted patterns, began to see a method in the madness.

“He’s used the city itself as a lockbox\!” Fimble declared one evening, hunched over the journal in their slightly-less-reeking-than-before office above The Salty Swab. “Landmarks, street names, guild symbols… they’re all part of the code\! And this key…” He held up the intricately carved wooden key. “It doesn’t just open doors, lads\! It resonates with specific locations, places where the other Eyes, or perhaps even the Stone of Golorr itself, might be hidden\!”

Their hunt for the Dragon Hoard became a city-wide scavenger hunt, a perilous game of cat and mouse played out against the backdrop of Waterdeep’s simmering faction wars. The key, guided by Fimble’s increasingly less-sputtering Illuminator, led them to a series of hidden locations: a forgotten crypt beneath a crumbling temple in the City of the Dead, where they faced spectral guardians and deciphered ancient riddles; a secret chamber behind a shifting bookcase in a dusty, long-abandoned wizard’s tower in the Castle Ward, where they narrowly avoided a magical trap that threatened to turn them into miniature, perpetually surprised, garden gnomes; and even, to Fitzwilliam’s profound horror, a brief, ill-advised foray back into the upper levels of Undermountain, following a resonance that led to a hidden duergar smuggling cache (which, unfortunately, contained more angry duergar than actual treasure).

With each location, they found not the hoard itself, but more clues, more pieces of the puzzle – cryptic messages, fragments of maps, and unsettlingly, evidence that other factions were also on the trail, their agents often mere steps behind, or ahead, of them. The Xanathar Guild, the Zhentarim, the enigmatic Unseen, and even, they began to suspect, agents of some of Waterdeep’s more ambitious noble houses, were all vying for the same prize.

Finally, after a series of harrowing near-misses, narrow escapes, and one particularly unfortunate incident involving a flock of aggressively territorial pigeons and a miscalibrated pie-flinging device (Fimble’s, naturally), the combined clues from the journal, the key’s resonance, and the two Eyes of Golorr led them to a specific, unassuming location in the Trades Ward: a modest, slightly dilapidated storefront that had once housed a renowned cartographer, now shuttered and seemingly abandoned.

“This is it,” Fimble breathed, his Illuminator humming with a quiet, satisfied intensity. “The resonance is… undeniable\! The final Eye, or perhaps the Stone of Golorr itself, is within\! And according to Lord Neverember’s cryptic notations… ‘Where maps are made, and secrets unmade, the greatest treasure lies in shade’… this must be the entrance to the Vault\!”

The storefront was locked, its windows boarded up. But as Pipkin held the wooden key towards the unassuming wooden door, the symbols on the key began to glow, mirroring faint, almost invisible lines that appeared on the door itself, forming the outline of a hidden, far more ancient, portal. The Vault’s location, or at least, a significant part of it, was finally within their grasp. But they knew, with a chilling certainty, that they were not the only ones who had followed the trail. The final race for the Dragon Hoard was about to begin.

##### **Chapter 10: Confrontation in the City**

(Master Draft)

The unassuming cartographer’s shop in the Trades Ward, its windows boarded, its door seemingly locked against the world, pulsed with a faint, almost imperceptible magical energy that only those attuned to such things, or those possessing a very specific, intricately carved wooden key, might notice. As Pipkin Tealeaf held their key towards the weathered wood of the main door, the symbols on its surface glowed with a soft, ethereal light, mirroring a series of intricate, almost invisible lines that shimmered into existence on the door itself, forming the outline of an ancient, hidden portal.

“This is it, lads,” Fimble Cogspinner whispered, his gnomish eyes wide with a mixture of scientific excitement and dawning apprehension. His Calibrated Pathway Illuminator hummed with a quiet, insistent intensity, its needle pointing directly at the glowing portal. “The primary access point to the Vault of Dragons\! Or at least, a significant, and undoubtedly heavily guarded, antechamber\!”

Renaer Neverember, his face a mask of grim determination, his hand resting on the hilt of his rapier, nodded slowly. “My father’s legacy… his secrets… they lie beyond that door. And perhaps, my friend Floon.” He still held onto the hope that Floon Blagmaar, captured by the Xanathar Guild weeks ago, might be found within the Vault, a prisoner or a pawn in this city-wide game of shadows and gold.

Fitzwilliam Tealeaf, however, was less focused on legacies and lost friends, and more on the very distinct, very unsettling feeling of being watched. “Pip,” he hissed, his voice a low, nervous tremor, “I don’t like this. It’s too quiet. Too… exposed. If we know this is the place, you can bet your last stale biscuit those Xanathar louts, or the Zhentarim sneaks, or those creepy Unseen floaty-types, know it too.”

His words were prophetic. As Kaelen, decades later when piecing together the convoluted history of these events, would learn from various, often contradictory, Waterdavian chronicles, the convergence upon the cartographer’s shop was less a coincidence and more an inevitability. The various factions, each with their own spies, their own informants, their own methods of persuasion and coercion, had been tracking the Eyes of Golorr, and those who possessed them, with a relentless, obsessive focus. The Tealeaf brothers, for all their attempts at discretion (which usually involved tripping over their own feet and accidentally setting fire to things), had left a trail of chaotic breadcrumbs that even the most inept faction agent could follow.

As Pipkin inserted the wooden key into the glowing outline on the door, as the ancient portal began to groan and rumble, a sound like stones grinding in the belly of some forgotten beast, the shadows in the surrounding alleyways and rooftops began to move.

The Xanathar Guild, led by the hulking, one-eyed bugbear they had so memorably encountered in the Gralhund Villa basement (who seemed to have developed a particular, and entirely understandable, grudge against small, pie-obsessed halflings), emerged from a nearby darkened warehouse, their crude weapons held ready, their beholder-eye tattoos gleaming with malevolent intent.

Simultaneously, Zhentarim agents, their black cloaks swirling like predatory shadows, materialized from the rooftops, their crossbows aimed, their expressions cold and calculating. The tall, wiry woman with eyes like chips of obsidian, who had confronted them in the alley behind The Gilded Gryphon, was among them, her thin-lipped smile a chilling promise of violence.

And from the street itself, seemingly coalescing from the very cobblestones and gaslight shadows, came the Unseen. Their cloaked and hooded forms glided with an unnerving silence, their presence a palpable wave of menace. The tall, imposing figure with the silver-tipped cane tapped it once, sharply, on the ground, a silent command that sent a shiver down Fitzwilliam’s spine.

Even a contingent of City Watch guards, drawn by the escalating faction tensions and the undeniable aura of illicit activity that clung to the cartographer’s shop like cheap perfume, arrived, their halberds forming a hesitant, increasingly bewildered, perimeter.

Pipkin, Fitzwilliam, Renaer, and Fimble found themselves in the epicenter of a multi-factional maelstrom, the glowing portal at their backs, a wall of hostile, determined faces before them, all eyes fixed on the prize they now seemed to possess: the key to Lord Neverember’s legendary hoard.

“Well, fiddle-dee-dee,” Pipkin muttered, his earlier excitement rapidly evaporating, replaced by a familiar, sinking feeling of impending, and likely very painful, doom. “Looks like the party’s started without us. And everyone, it seems, wants an invitation to our little vault-opening ceremony.”

The confrontation in the heart of Waterdeep, the final, desperate scramble for the Dragon Hoard, had begun. And the Tealeaf brothers, as usual, were caught squarely, and entirely out of their depth, in the middle of it.

##### **Chapter 11: The Weight of Coin**

(Master Draft)

The scene outside the glowing portal of the cartographer’s shop was a tableau of greed, ambition, and rapidly escalating violence. Xanathar Guild thugs, Zhentarim agents, and the enigmatic Unseen operatives faced off in a tense, three-way standoff, their shared desire for the Dragon Hoard momentarily outweighing their usual animosities, but the air crackled with unspoken threats and the promise of imminent, brutal conflict. The City Watch, outnumbered and clearly bewildered by the sheer audacity of this multi-factional confrontation in the heart of their city, struggled to maintain even a semblance of order.

And in the midst of it all, Pipkin and Fitzwilliam Tealeaf, along with their increasingly exasperated companions Renaer Neverember and Fimble Cogspinner, found themselves holding the literal key to this powder keg.

“Right then, lads,” Pipkin squeaked, his voice a mixture of terror and a dawning, reckless bravado. He looked at the glowing portal behind them, then at the wall of hostile faces before them. “Looks like we’ve got a choice. Get carved up like a holiday roast by this lot, or take our chances with whatever’s behind that rather ominous-looking door. Personally, I’m leaning towards the door. At least there might be pie in there.”

Before anyone could offer a counter-argument (or a sensible escape plan, which seemed to be in remarkably short supply), the Zhentarim made their move. The tall, wiry woman with the obsidian eyes barked a sharp command, and her agents surged forward, their blades flashing, their movements precise and deadly, aiming to secure the portal and the halflings who held the key.

The Xanathar Guild thugs, never ones to be outdone in matters of unsubtle violence, roared their defiance and charged, their crude weapons swinging, turning the narrow street into a maelstrom of clashing steel and guttural war cries. The Unseen agents, their forms flickering like shadows, moved with an unnerving silence, their attacks targeted and lethal, seeking to exploit the chaos to their own advantage.

The City Watch, caught in the crossfire, blew their whistles with a frantic, futile urgency, their attempts to restore order swallowed by the erupting faction war.

“The portal, lads\! Now\!” Fimble shrieked, his Calibrated Pathway Illuminator spinning wildly, emitting a series of high-pitched, frantic beeps that added to the general cacophony. He grabbed Renaer’s arm, tugging him towards the glowing doorway.

Pipkin and Fitzwilliam needed no further encouragement. With a shared yelp of terror, they scrambled through the portal, the lead-lined box containing the Eyes of Golorr clutched tightly between them, the sounds of the escalating street battle fading behind them as the ancient gateway began to rumble and groan, its light intensifying, its magical energies swirling around them.

They found themselves in a vast, circular chamber, its walls lined with colossal stone vaults, each sealed with a heavy, rune-etched door. The air here was cool, still, and hummed with a faint, ancient magic. In the center of the chamber, upon a raised obsidian dais, rested a single, unassuming stone chest. The Dragon Hoard. Or at least, a significant portion of it.

But they were not alone. Standing before the chest, his back to them, was a figure they recognized with a jolt of horrified disbelief: the shadowy, cloaked agent of the Unseen who had snatched the first wooden box from them in the alleyway near the Dock Ward. He had, it seemed, found his own way into the Vault.

The Unseen agent turned slowly, the wooden box they had sought – the one containing the Stone of Golorr, as they now realized – held in his hand. From the depths of his hood, a silken, chilling voice whispered, “So, the little halflings have finally arrived. And they have brought the Eyes. How… convenient.”

Before Pipkin or Fitzwilliam could react, before Renaer could demand the return of his father’s legacy, the Unseen agent raised the Stone of Golorr. It pulsed with a dark, malevolent light, its three empty eye sockets seeming to hunger for the wooden Eyes the halflings carried. The lead-lined box in Pipkin’s hands grew suddenly, intensely cold, the Eyes within vibrating with a strange, resonant energy.

It was at this moment, as the Unseen agent prepared to unite the Stone and its Eyes, as the fate of the Dragon Hoard hung in the balance, that the portal behind them, the one through which they had just escaped, shimmered and solidified, its light fading. And then, with a final, grinding groan, it vanished, leaving behind only a blank, unyielding stone wall.

They were trapped. Trapped in the Vault of Dragons with a powerful, enigmatic agent of the Unseen, who now held the key to unleashing the Stone of Golorr’s full, terrifying power.

The weight of the coin, the allure of the Dragon Hoard, had led Pipkin and Fitzwilliam Tealeaf into their most dangerous, and potentially most catastrophic, misadventure yet. And as the Unseen agent’s chilling laughter echoed through the silent, treasure-filled vault, they had the distinct, sinking feeling that their dreams of pies and a less leaky roof were about to be replaced by a reality far more complex, far more perilous, and far less conducive to a long and prosperous halfling lifespan. The hunt for the hoard was over. The true confrontation, in the shadowy heart of Waterdeep’s deepest secrets, had just begun.

### **Book 6: Beyond the Second Sundering: Thread of Gate, Thread of Flame**

#### **Series Title: Chronicles of the Fifth Age: Tales From Beyond the Second Sundering**

#### **Part 1: Descent into Avernus**

##### **Chapter 1: The Fall of Elturel**

(Master Draft)

The Year of Three Ships Sailing (1492 DR) was etching its name into the annals of Faerûn with a series of calamities that seemed to herald an age of unprecedented despair. While the death curse in Chult had finally been broken by Elara and Torvin, and the shadows of elemental evil in the Dessarin Valley had been temporarily pushed back by the combined efforts of Kaelen, Rhys, and Anya Faelar, a new, terrifying darkness was gathering in the lands bordering the Western Heartlands.

Kaelen and Rhys, having parted ways with their companions in Red Larch after the Fane of the Eye’s unsettling silence, had journeyed towards Elturel, the holy city of the Companion. Their purpose was twofold: Kaelen sought counsel from the devout priests of Torm and Lathander, hoping to understand the lingering echoes of the Elder Elemental Eye and the disquieting whispers Jergal had imparted regarding a "price for every soul restored." Rhys, ever pragmatic, sought news of the wider world, and perhaps, a chance to aid communities still reeling from the chaos of the preceding years. The city of Elturel, with its ever-present, radiant second sun known as the Companion, had long stood as a beacon of hope and divine protection in a world often shadowed by evil.

They arrived on a day that began like any other in the holy city. The Companion bathed the fields of Elturgard in its warm, golden light. The devout went about their prayers, the markets bustled with activity, and the Hellriders, Elturel’s famed cavalry, patrolled the city walls, their armor gleaming. Yet, beneath the surface of this idyllic scene, Kaelen sensed a subtle wrongness, a discordant note in the symphony of divine radiance. The light of the Companion, though brilliant, felt… strained, its warmth carrying an almost imperceptible, underlying chill. Jergal’s presence in his mind was a cold, expectant silence.

The calamity struck with a speed and ferocity that defied comprehension. The sky above Elturel, moments before a clear, azure canvas, was ripped asunder. Not by storm clouds, but by a tear in the very fabric of reality, a bleeding wound that oozed a sickly, crimson light and the stench of brimstone. From this infernal rift, colossal chains of black, hell-forged iron, each link larger than a warhorse, descended upon the city, their unholy clanging a death knell that drowned out the screams of the terrified populace.

The Companion, the radiant second sun that had protected Elturel for generations, sputtered, its golden light dimming, then shifting to a baleful, blood-red hue. Its divine warmth vanished, replaced by an infernal heat that scorched the air and withered the very stones of the city. The ground beneath Elturel groaned, then began to rise, not in a natural earthquake, but as if torn from the earth by an unseen, malevolent hand.

Kaelen and Rhys, caught in the heart of the city as chaos erupted, fought with a desperate, hopeless courage. Kaelen’s greatsword, Whisper, its shadowy aura a stark contrast to the infernal light, carved a path through panicked crowds and the first, tentative incursions of fiendish creatures – spined devils, their forms wreathed in shadow and flame, that began to pour from the rift. Rhys, his arrows finding their mark against these otherworldly horrors, tried to rally the city’s overwhelmed defenders, his voice a clarion call amidst the terror.

But it was a futile gesture. The city of Elturel, lifted from the earth by the unholy chains, its Companion now a beacon of damnation rather than salvation, was being dragged, inexorably, into the infernal rift, disappearing into the fiery, smoke-choked depths of what could only be Avernus, the first layer of the Nine Hells.

The screams of tens of thousands of souls, men, women, and children, were swallowed by the rift as Elturel vanished from the Material Plane, leaving behind only a gaping, smoking wound in the earth and a sky stained with the crimson light of damnation. Kaelen and Rhys, standing on the very precipice of this abyss, their faces illuminated by the hellish glow, could only watch in stunned, horrified disbelief as an entire city, a beacon of hope and faith, was dragged into hell.

The whispers of Jergal in Kaelen’s mind were no longer silent. They were a cold, chilling pronouncement: *“The pact is sealed. The price is paid. Avernus claims its due.”*

The fall of Elturel was not just a tragedy; it was a statement, a terrifying demonstration of infernal power, a harbinger of a new, even more desperate, struggle against the forces of the Nine Hells. And Kaelen and Rhys, witnesses to this cataclysm, knew, with a chilling certainty, that their path now led downwards, into the fiery depths of Avernus itself.

##### **Chapter 2: A City in Chains**

(Master Draft)

The smoldering crater where Elturel had once stood was a scene of unimaginable desolation. The air reeked of sulfur and burnt earth, the ground still radiated an infernal heat, and the sky above was a permanent, angry scar, a reminder of the city’s horrific, otherworldly abduction. Refugees, their faces masks of shock and despair, streamed away from the abyss, their tales of the city’s fall spreading like wildfire across the Western Heartlands, carrying with them a new, potent wave of fear.

Kaelen and Rhys, having narrowly escaped being dragged into Avernus with Elturel, made their way to Baldur’s Gate, the sprawling, corrupt metropolis on the Sword Coast. The city, already a viper’s nest of political intrigue, criminal ambition, and social unrest, was now teetering on the brink of chaos. The news of Elturel’s fall had sent shockwaves through its populace. Fear of a similar fate, or perhaps, of an infernal invasion, gripped the city. And the influx of desperate, traumatized Elturian refugees was straining its already overburdened resources, fanning the flames of social unrest and xenophobia.

Baldur’s Gate, a city perpetually in chains – chains of avarice, of corruption, of a rigid, unyielding patriar system – now faced a new, more literal threat. Cultists, their faces hidden behind grotesque, fiendish masks, began to appear in the city’s shadowed alleyways, their whispers promising power and salvation to the desperate, their actions sowing discord and violence. These were not the disorganized, fanatical cultists of the Dragon Queen or the elemental prophets; these were disciplined, cunning agents of Asmodeus, of Zariel, the Archduchess of Avernus, their purpose clear: to destabilize Baldur’s Gate, to weaken its defenses, to prepare it for its own, inevitable descent into the Nine Hells.

Kaelen, his connection to Jergal providing him with chilling insights into the infernal machinations at play, recognized the signs. The fall of Elturel was not an isolated event; it was part of a larger, far more insidious plan, a diabolical scheme to drag entire cities, entire nations, into the endless, bloody conflict of the Blood War. Jergal's whispers spoke of an ancient pact, of a city sold for power, of a debt finally, terribly, called due.

Rhys, his hunter’s instincts recoiling from the unnatural corruption that now permeated Baldur’s Gate, found himself navigating a new, urban wilderness, its dangers as deadly, if more subtle, than any he had faced in the frozen peaks of the Spine of the World or the blighted depths of the Dessarin Valley. He saw the fear in the eyes of the common folk, the desperation that made them susceptible to the cultists’ poisonous promises, the avarice that blinded the city’s powerful patriars to the true nature of the threat.

Their investigation into the Elturel crisis, and the cultists who seemed to be exploiting it, led them into the city’s darkest corners: the smoke-filled taverns of the Lower City, where desperate refugees whispered tales of fiendish deals and infernal pacts; the opulent, decadent manors of the Upper City, where ambitious nobles played dangerous games with forces they did not comprehend; and the labyrinthine, shadowed network of the Guild, Waterdeep’s notorious thieves’ organization, whose tendrils reached into every aspect of the city’s life, and whose leaders, it was rumored, had their own, vested interest in the city’s potential damnation.

They encountered remnants of the Hellriders, Elturel’s once-proud cavalry, now broken, scattered, their faith shattered, their purpose lost. Some had fallen into despair, drowning their sorrows in cheap ale and bitter recriminations. Others, their eyes burning with a desperate, fanatical light, had joined the infernal cults, believing Zariel’s power offered the only hope for their lost city, or perhaps, for their own salvation.

It was from one such fallen Hellrider, a man named Reya Mantlemorn, her youthful face etched with a grief and fury that belied her years, that Kaelen and Rhys learned the horrifying truth behind Elturel’s damnation. She spoke of Thavius Kreeg, Elturel’s High Overseer, a man revered as a savior, the one who had brought forth the Companion and protected the city for generations. But his protection, Reya revealed with a voice choked with betrayal, had come at a terrible price: an infernal pact, signed in blood, that had traded Elturel’s soul, and the souls of its people, for fifty years of false security. The pact was now due. And Zariel had come to collect.

Reya, her heart burning with a desperate need for vengeance and a fragile hope of redeeming her lost city, pleaded with Kaelen and Rhys to help her. She spoke of a way to follow Elturel into Avernus, of a chance, however slim, to break the pact, to free the city from its infernal chains.

Baldur’s Gate was a city teetering on the brink, its own damnation seemingly inevitable. But for Kaelen and Rhys, the path was clear. Elturel’s fate was a chilling warning, a grim portent of what awaited if Zariel’s ambitions were not checked. Their journey into the Nine Hells, into the fiery, blood-soaked plains of Avernus, was no longer a matter of choice, but of desperate, grim necessity.

##### **Chapter 3: Infernal Bargains**

(Master Draft)

The revelation of Thavius Kreeg’s infernal pact and Elturel’s horrific descent had solidified Kaelen and Rhys’s resolve. They would journey into Avernus, not just to witness the fate of the fallen city, but to find a way to break Zariel’s chains, to offer some measure of hope to its damned populace, and perhaps, to prevent Baldur’s Gate from suffering a similar, terrible destiny. Reya Mantlemorn, the young, grief-stricken Hellrider, became their unlikely guide and ally, her knowledge of Elturel’s history and her burning desire for vengeance a potent, if volatile, addition to their small, desperate fellowship.

Their first challenge was finding a reliable means of reaching Avernus. The rift above Elturel’s crater was a one-way passage, a gaping maw that swallowed souls but offered no return. They needed a guide, a gate, a specific infernal contract that might grant them passage. The cultists of Zariel operating within Baldur’s Gate held some of these keys, their profane rituals and whispered incantations capable of tearing temporary rents in the fabric of reality, creating fleeting portals to the Nine Hells.

Their investigation led them into the city’s darkest, most corrupt corners, to clandestine meetings in shadowed warehouses, to secret shrines hidden beneath crumbling temples, to opulent manors where decadent nobles dabbled in forbidden magics, their souls already forfeit in exchange for fleeting power or perverse pleasures. The cultists they encountered were a chilling new breed of evil: not the frothing fanatics of Tiamat or the deranged zealots of the elemental princes, but cold, calculating individuals, their eyes burning with an intelligent, infernal light, their words honeyed promises that masked a core of absolute, soul-chilling malevolence. They spoke of Zariel not as a distant, terrifying deity, but as a pragmatic leader, a fallen angel whose ambition offered a new, terrible order in a world teetering on the brink of chaos.

Kaelen, his connection to Jergal providing him with a unique insight into the intricate, deadly dance of infernal politics, navigated these treacherous encounters with a cold, wary pragmatism. He understood the language of devils, the currency of souls, the subtle art of the infernal bargain. He knew that information, passage, even temporary alliances, could be bought, but always at a terrible price, a price often measured in fractions of one’s soul, or in promises that bound one to an eternity of damnation. Jergal’s whispers were a constant, chilling reminder of the stakes, of the slippery slope that led to infernal servitude.

Rhys, his hunter’s instincts ill-suited to this world of shadowy intrigue and silken deceptions, found himself relying on Kaelen’s grim expertise, his own straightforward honesty often a liability in these encounters. He observed the desperate, broken souls who had made pacts with devils, their eyes hollow, their spirits consumed, and his heart ached with a mixture of pity and a cold, righteous anger.

They encountered a diabolical Tiamat cultist cell, led by a charismatic, if utterly depraved, individual named Arkhan the Cruel, a dragonborn paladin whose oath was pledged not to any god of justice, but to the five-headed Queen of Evil Dragons herself. Arkhan, it was rumored, possessed an artifact capable of tearing open temporary gates to Avernus, an artifact he occasionally used to traffic souls or to summon fiendish allies. The encounter with Arkhan and his fanatical followers was a brutal, bloody affair, fought in the desecrated ruins of a once-holy temple, Kaelen’s Whisper and Rhys’s arrows clashing against dragon-forged steel and unholy magic. They managed to defeat Arkhan’s lieutenants and disrupt his profane rituals, but Arkhan himself, powerful and cunning, escaped, vowing a terrible vengeance. They did, however, secure a fragment of his infernal gate-key, a piece of a larger puzzle.

Their path also led them to a more enigmatic, and perhaps even more dangerous, figure: Sylvira Savikas, a tiefling expert in infernal lore, a scholar of Candlekeep whose knowledge of the Nine Hells was as vast as it was unsettling. Sylvira, her eyes burning with an almost feverish intensity, her chambers filled with forbidden texts and strange, arcane devices, confirmed their worst fears about Elturel’s pact and Zariel’s ambitions. She also possessed a crucial piece of information: the location of a permanent, if heavily guarded, portal to Avernus, hidden deep within the ruins of a forgotten temple in the Fields of the Dead, a desolate wasteland haunted by the ghosts of ancient battles.

But Sylvira’s aid came with a price. She tasked them with recovering a specific artifact from Avernus, an infernal puzzle box said to contain the contract that bound Elturel to Zariel. If they could retrieve the box, Sylvira believed she might be able to find a way to break the contract, to free the city from its infernal chains. It was a desperate hope, a fragile thread in a tapestry of damnation, but it was enough.

The infernal bargains were struck, the alliances, however tenuous, forged. Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya, armed with Sylvira’s knowledge, the fragmented gate-key from Arkhan’s cult, and a grim, desperate resolve, prepared for their descent. The road to hell was paved not with good intentions, but with broken pacts, stolen souls, and the chilling certainty that they were walking into a realm from which few, if any, ever returned.

##### **Chapter 4: The Road to Hell**

(Master Draft)

The Fields of the Dead, a vast, windswept plain west of Baldur’s Gate, lived up to its grim name. It was a desolate landscape, haunted by the lingering echoes of countless ancient battles, its soil stained with the blood of forgotten armies, its air thick with a palpable sense of sorrow and unrest. Whispering winds carried the mournful cries of unseen spirits, and at night, spectral warriors were said to rise from their shallow graves, re-enacting their final, futile struggles. It was here, amidst the crumbling ruins of a forgotten temple dedicated to some long-dead god of war, that Sylvira Savikas had told them they would find the portal to Avernus.

The journey across the Fields of the Dead was a somber, unnerving experience. Kaelen, his connection to Jergal making him particularly sensitive to the lingering emanations of death and despair, felt the weight of ages pressing down on him, the silent screams of a thousand lost souls echoing in his mind. Rhys, his hunter’s senses on high alert, scanned the desolate landscape for any sign of danger – not just the spectral remnants of fallen warriors, but also the more tangible threat of roving bandits, desperate refugees, or the infernal cultists who might still be lurking in the region, guarding the secrets of their dark masters. Reya Mantlemorn, her youthful face a mask of grim determination, her hand rarely straying far from the hilt of her Hellrider’s longsword, walked with a heavy heart, the ghosts of her fallen comrades seeming to whisper to her from the wind-swept grasses.

They found the ruined temple as Sylvira had described, a crumbling edifice of black, obsidian-like stone, its once-proud towers now shattered and broken, its courtyards overgrown with thorned vines and skeletal trees. The air around it was cold, unnaturally still, and pulsed with a faint, nauseating aura of infernal energy. The ground was littered with the bones of fallen warriors, and strange, unsettling symbols, etched in what looked like dried blood, adorned the temple’s remaining walls – symbols Kaelen recognized from Arkhan the Cruel’s desecrated shrine, sigils of Asmodeus, of Zariel, of the Nine Hells.

The portal itself was hidden deep within the temple’s catacombs, in a circular chamber whose walls were carved with scenes of fiendish triumph and mortal despair. It was not a shimmering gateway of light, but a gaping, shadowy maw, a tear in the fabric of reality that seemed to suck the very light and warmth from the air, its depths a swirling vortex of crimson and black, promising only pain and oblivion. Before it, a contingent of Zariel’s cultists, their faces hidden behind fiendish masks, their eyes burning with an unholy light, stood guard, their profane chants echoing through the subterranean chamber, their purpose clear: to protect this unholy gateway, and to offer up any unfortunate intruders as sacrifices to their infernal mistress.

The battle for the portal was desperate and brutal. Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya fought with a coordinated fury, their blades and arrows a storm of righteous anger against the cultists’ unholy magic and fiendishly-forged weapons. Kaelen’s Whisper, wreathed in shadows, seemed to drink the infernal light, its edge finding purchase against the cultists’ enchanted armor. Rhys’s arrows, each one loosed with a silent prayer to whatever gods of justice still listened, found their mark with deadly precision, disrupting the cultists’ profane rituals, silencing their blasphemous chants. Reya, her Hellrider’s training taking over, fought with the ferocity of a cornered lioness, her longsword a silver flash, her war cries a defiant challenge to the forces of damnation.

They defeated the cultists, their bodies collapsing in heaps of dark robes and shattered masks, their unholy fires extinguished. But the portal remained, a gaping, hungry wound in the world, its crimson light pulsing with a malevolent, irresistible pull.

There was no turning back. With a shared, grim nod, Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya stepped across the threshold, plunging into the swirling, fiery vortex. The transition was a jarring, sickening wrench, a tearing of senses, a cacophony of screams and tormented whispers, a blast of infernal heat that scorched their lungs and seared their souls.

And then, they were through.

They stood on a precipice of black, obsidian-like rock, overlooking a landscape of unimaginable horror. The sky above was a perpetual, roiling storm of fire and ash, illuminated by flashes of crimson lightning and the distant, baleful glow of infernal citadels. The air was thick with the stench of sulfur, burning flesh, and ancient, unending despair. Below them, stretching as far as the eye could see, were the blood-soaked plains of Avernus, the first layer of the Nine Hells, a battlefield littered with the wreckage of countless wars, the bones of fallen angels and forgotten demons, and the souls of the damned, their tormented forms writhing in an eternal agony. In the distance, impossibly, horrifically, they saw it: the city of Elturel, chained and broken, hanging suspended above a lake of fire, its Companion now a captive, dying star, its light a pale, sorrowful echo of its former glory.

The road to hell had been traversed. Their journey into the heart of damnation, their desperate quest to save a fallen city and perhaps, to redeem a fallen angel, had truly begun. The weight of Avernus, its ancient evil, its unending despair, settled upon them like a physical blow, threatening to crush their spirits before their quest had even started. But amidst the horror, a flicker of defiance, of hope, however fragile, still burned within their hearts. They were mortals in hell, facing impossible odds, but they were not yet broken. And in Avernus, the land of lost souls and broken oaths, even the smallest spark of courage could, perhaps, ignite a flame of redemption.

### **Book 6: Beyond the Second Sundering: Thread of Gate, Thread of Flame**

#### **Series Title: Chronicles of the Fifth Age: Tales From Beyond the Second Sundering**

#### **Part 2: The Wastes of Avernus**

##### **Chapter 5: The Blood War's Front Lines**

(Master Draft)

Avernus. The first layer of the Nine Hells, a realm of eternal, brutal conflict, stretched before Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya Mantlemorn as a landscape of unimaginable horror. The sky was a roiling storm of fire and ash, casting a lurid, blood-red glow upon plains littered with the wreckage of infernal war machines, the bones of fallen angels and forgotten demons, and rivers of molten iron that carved fiery scars across the blasted, obsidian-like rock. The air itself was thick with the stench of sulfur, burning flesh, and an ancient, all-pervasive despair that sought to crush the spirit and extinguish hope.

Elturel, the city they had witnessed being dragged into this hellscape, hung suspended in the fiery sky above a vast, churning lake of magma, chained like a beast awaiting slaughter, its divine Companion now a dying, captive star, its light a pale, sorrowful mockery of its former glory. The distant, tormented screams of its trapped populace were a constant, chilling reminder of the stakes of their desperate, seemingly impossible mission.

Their immediate surroundings, the precipice of black rock upon which the portal from the Fields of the Dead had deposited them, offered little comfort. Below lay a vast, desolate plain, a staging ground for the endless Blood War between devils and demons. Legions of spined devils, their forms wreathed in shadow and flame, marched in disciplined, terrifying formations, their barbed spears glinting in the infernal light. Hulking, brutish demons, their forms a chaotic fusion of mismatched limbs and razor-sharp claws, surged across the battlefield, their roars a cacophony of mindless rage. And in the midst of it all, infernal war machines – monstrous contraptions of blackened steel, belching smoke and fire, their wheels crushing bone and rock with equal indifference – thundered across the plains, their purpose singular: to reap a bloody harvest of souls.

“By the Light of Lathander…” Reya breathed, her youthful face pale with a mixture of horror and a dawning, terrible understanding. This was the fate of her city, her people. This was the realm of Zariel, the fallen angel, the Archduchess of Avernus, the architect of Elturel’s damnation. Her Hellrider’s bravado faltered, replaced by a profound, soul-deep despair.

Kaelen, his hand resting on the hilt of Whisper, his gaze sweeping across the infernal vista, felt a cold, grim resolve settle upon him. Jergal’s presence in his mind was a silent, unwavering judgment, a reminder that even in this realm of absolute evil, fate’s threads still twisted, choices still mattered, and the balance, however precariously, could still be shifted. “This is Zariel’s domain,” he stated, his voice low and steady, cutting through Reya’s despair. “And Elturel… is her prize. We need to find a way to it. And a way to break those chains.”

Rhys, his hunter’s instincts screaming at him to flee this unnatural, terrifying landscape, forced himself to remain calm, to assess their situation. Their portal back to the Material Plane was gone. They were stranded in hell, with no clear path, no allies, and enemies beyond counting. “We need shelter,” he said, his voice tight. “And information. This… this is a battlefield. We won’t survive long out in the open.”

Their descent from the precipice onto the plains of Avernus was a perilous undertaking. They navigated treacherous, narrow pathways of crumbling obsidian, the ground beneath their feet often slick with blood or ichor. They evaded patrols of leering imps and barbed devils, their movements silent and desperate, Kaelen’s shadow abilities and Rhys’s woodcraft, surprisingly effective even in this hellish landscape, their only shield.

They found temporary, if dubious, shelter in the hollowed-out carcass of a colossal, long-dead infernal beast, its ribcage a grotesque, makeshift cave. The air within reeked of decay and brimstone, but it offered a momentary respite from the watchful eyes of Avernus’s myriad predators.

It was here, amidst the bones of a forgotten horror, that they encountered their first potential, if utterly untrustworthy, ally. A small, cackling imp, its skin the color of dried blood, its eyes burning with a malicious, opportunistic light, materialized from the shadows. It introduced itself as Squeeb, a purveyor of information, a dealer in souls, a survivor in a realm where survival was a commodity bought and sold at a terrible price.

“Lost, are we, little mortals?” Squeeb cackled, its voice like nails on a slate. “Strayed a bit far from your cozy, sun-warmed world, have you? Avernus is… unforgiving… to the uninvited.” It eyed their weapons, their travel-stained clothes, the desperate resolve in their eyes. “But perhaps… perhaps Squeeb can help. For a price, of course. Information is never free in the Nine Hells. Especially information that might keep you… un-damned… for a little while longer.”

The imp spoke of Lulu, a tiny, hollyphant (a miniature, winged elephant-like celestial) with a fragmented memory, who was rumored to have once been Zariel’s companion, back when the Archduchess was still an angel of light. Lulu, Squeeb hinted, might hold the key to understanding Zariel’s fall, the nature of Elturel’s pact, and perhaps, a way to redeem them both. But Lulu was lost, her mind shattered, her whereabouts unknown, a fleeting, almost mythical figure in the blood-soaked tapestry of Avernus.

The Blood War’s front lines were a brutal, unforgiving crucible. Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya, guided by Squeeb’s dubious directions and their own desperate hope, began their search for Lulu, their path leading them deeper into the heart of Avernus, into a realm where every shadow concealed a new horror, every encounter a potential betrayal, and every choice a step closer to salvation, or eternal damnation.

##### **Chapter 6: Infernal War Machines**

(Master Draft)

The plains of Avernus were a vast, desolate expanse, a canvas of shattered rock and rivers of fire, constantly reshaped by the eternal, brutal conflict of the Blood War. Traversing this hellscape on foot was a slow, perilous undertaking, each step a gamble against patrols of devils, hordes of demons, and the very land itself, which seemed to actively seek their destruction. Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya Mantlemorn, their search for the hollyphant Lulu a desperate, almost quixotic quest, soon realized they needed a more efficient, and perhaps more defensible, means of travel.

The answer lay in the infernal war machines. These monstrous contraptions, cobbled together from blackened steel, jagged bone, and captured souls that shrieked in eternal torment from within their infernal engines, thundered across the Avernian wastes, their purpose singular: to reap a bloody harvest in the endless war. They came in all shapes and sizes, from nimble, scorpion-like Devil’s Rides that skittered across the plains on articulated legs, to massive, fortress-like Demon Grinders, their colossal wheels crushing everything in their path, their hulls bristling with barbed harpoons and soul-burning cannons.

Acquiring such a machine was a feat that bordered on suicidal. They were heavily armed, crewed by battle-hardened devils or ruthless mortal cultists who had sold their souls for a taste of infernal power, and often fueled by the very essence of captured souls, their engines screaming with an unholy, life-draining energy.

Their first attempt, a daring raid on a lone Devil’s Ride that had strayed from its patrol, ended in a chaotic, fire-laced retreat. Kaelen’s shadowy attacks and Rhys’s arrows managed to disable its crew, but the machine itself, its infernal engine sputtering and dying, proved too complex, too alien, for them to control. They were forced to abandon it as a larger patrol of barbed devils, drawn by the sounds of battle, converged on their position.

Their salvation, and their first true taste of infernal vehicular mayhem, came from an unexpected, and thoroughly disreputable, source: Mad Maggie.

Squeeb, the opportunistic imp, for a further, exorbitant price (payable in promises of future souls, a currency Kaelen found increasingly, chillingly, easy to offer in this desperate realm), led them to Fort Knucklebone. This ramshackle, fortified junkyard, built from the skeletal remains of colossal infernal beasts and the cannibalized hulls of wrecked war machines, served as the domain of Mad Maggie, a night hag whose mechanical genius was matched only by her grotesque appearance and her insatiable appetite for misery and despair.

Mad Maggie, her skin the color of bruised plums, her eyes gleaming with a malicious, knowing light, her workshop filled with a bewildering array of infernal contraptions, spare parts, and tormented souls trapped in jars like grotesque, glowing fireflies, greeted them with a cackling, toothless grin. She was surrounded by a bizarre retinue of redcaps – small, vicious fey creatures who reveled in bloodshed and mischief – and a small army of imps and quasits who scurried about, tending to her infernal forges and her even more infernal experiments.

“Well, well,” Mad Maggie cackled, her voice like grinding gears. “More lost little lambs, strayed from the mortal flock, have we? Come to Mad Maggie for a bit of infernal engineering, have you? Looking for a ride through the pretty, pretty fires of Avernus?” She eyed their battered forms, their desperate eyes, with a keen, appraising interest. “Maggie has rides, oh yes. Fast ones, mean ones, ones that scream with the voices of a thousand tortured souls. But Maggie’s rides… they don’t come cheap, my pretties. Everything in Avernus has a price.”

The price, as always, was steep. Not just coin, which was almost worthless in this realm, but favors, promises, pieces of their past, fragments of their hope. Kaelen, his heart a cold stone in his chest, bargained with the night hag, his words careful, his offers precise, Jergal’s detached counsel a chilling guide through the treacherous currents of infernal negotiation. He offered her information about the surface world, about the elemental cults, about the shattered Ordning – knowledge that Maggie, with her vast network of soul-trading contacts, might find… useful. He offered her promises of future service, vague enough to perhaps be circumvented, yet specific enough to satisfy her immediate, avaricious desires.

In return, Mad Maggie provided them with a salvaged, somewhat battered, but surprisingly functional Devil’s Ride. It was a crude, skeletal machine, its engine sputtering with a reluctant, infernal energy, its armor patched with mismatched plates of blackened steel, its weapons – a single, rust-eaten harpoon launcher and a pair of flame-belching nozzles – looking more dangerous to the crew than to any potential enemy. But it was a start. It was mobility. It was a chance.

Maggie also offered them a cryptic piece of advice regarding Lulu, the hollyphant they sought. “The little trumpeter… she has a broken heart, a broken mind. Lost in the mists of her own sorrow. But she remembers… fragments. Fragments of a sword. A blade of celestial light, once wielded by an angel. An angel who fell. Find the sword, my pretties, and perhaps… perhaps you will find the path to the hollyphant’s lost memories. And perhaps, just perhaps, the path to Elturel’s salvation.”

With their newly acquired, if somewhat dubious, infernal war machine, and Mad Maggie’s cryptic counsel echoing in their minds, Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya set out once more into the desolate, blood-soaked wastes of Avernus. Their journey was now a desperate race against time, a perilous navigation of a realm where devils, demons, and rogue war machines battled for supremacy, their path guided by the faint, almost forgotten, scent of a lost celestial, and the even fainter, more desperate, hope of redeeming a fallen city and a fallen angel.

##### **Chapter 7: Souls in Chains**

(Master Draft)

The Avernian wastes were a symphony of torment, a landscape sculpted by eons of infernal warfare and unending despair. As Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya Mantlemorn thundered across the blasted plains in their sputtering, jury-rigged Devil’s Ride, the search for Lulu, the amnesiac hollyphant, led them through scenes of unimaginable suffering. They witnessed firsthand the cruel economy of Avernus, a realm where souls were the ultimate currency, harvested like crops, traded like trinkets, and tormented for the amusement of their diabolical masters.

They saw legions of lemures, the lowest form of devil, their vaguely humanoid forms misshapen and wretched, their eyes vacant with an eternal, mindless agony, being herded across the plains by sneering, whip-cracking imps, driven towards infernal forges where their very essence would be smelted into the raw materials of war – weapons, armor, the very fuel that powered the infernal war machines.

They encountered chain devils, kytons, their bodies a grotesque fusion of flesh and rusted iron, their movements accompanied by the chilling clatter of the chains they wielded with such sadistic glee. These fiends specialized in the capture and torment of souls, their infernal prisons – cages of jagged bone, pillars of screaming flesh, rivers of molten despair – littering the Avernian landscape, each a monument to Zariel’s cruel, unyielding dominion.

In the shadow of a colossal, obsidian citadel that rose from the plains like a cancerous growth, its towers piercing the fiery sky, they stumbled upon a soul auction. Here, devils of various ranks – bearded devils, bone devils, even a stern, imperious erinyes whose dark beauty was as terrifying as it was captivating – bartered for the souls of mortals, their contracts written in blood on parchments of flayed skin, their bargains sealed with promises of power, pleasure, or a temporary respite from eternal torment. The souls themselves, ethereal, translucent figures, their faces masks of terror and despair, were displayed in shimmering, magical cages, their whimpers a faint, heartbreaking counterpoint to the devils’ harsh, guttural negotiations.

Reya Mantlemorn, her Hellrider’s heart aching with a mixture of fury and a profound, empathetic sorrow, found these scenes almost unbearable. She saw in the tormented faces of the damned the reflections of her own lost Elturian comrades, her family, her friends, their souls now trapped in this infernal realm, their fates perhaps even more terrible than she had imagined. Her desire for vengeance against Zariel warred with a growing, desperate need to find some way, any way, to alleviate their suffering, to offer them some measure of peace, however fleeting.

Kaelen, his gaze cold and assessing, observed the infernal economy with a detached pragmatism that masked a deeper, more unsettling understanding. Jergal’s whispers in his mind spoke of the intricate, terrible balance of the cosmos, of the endless cycle of souls, of the laws that governed even this realm of absolute evil. He knew that interfering in these transactions, attempting to free these souls, was a dangerous, perhaps even futile, gesture. Every soul in Avernus was bound by a contract, by a choice, however coerced or desperate, and the breaking of such pacts often carried consequences even more terrible than eternal damnation.

Rhys, his hunter’s instincts for justice and fairness deeply offended by this grotesque parody of commerce, struggled to contain his rage. He saw not just abstract souls, but individuals, their lives, their hopes, their dreams, all reduced to mere commodities in this infernal marketplace. He longed to unleash his arrows, to strike down these diabolical merchants, but Kaelen’s quiet, warning hand stayed his bow. Direct confrontation here, against such overwhelming power, would be suicide.

Their search for Lulu led them to a place known as the Stygian Dock, a fetid, bustling port on the banks of the River Styx, the infamous river of forgetfulness that flowed through the Lower Planes. Here, souls were loaded onto grim, bone-prowed barges, their destinations unknown, their fates sealed. It was rumored that Lulu, in her fragmented, sorrowful wanderings, often frequented this place, drawn by the mournful cries of the damned, her celestial nature a strange, discordant note in this symphony of despair.

And it was here, amidst the throngs of tormented souls and their diabolical handlers, that they finally found her. Lulu, the hollyphant, was a tiny, almost insignificant figure, her usually bright, golden fur matted and dull, her once-sparkling sapphire eyes clouded with an ancient, unbearable sorrow. She hovered near a group of newly arrived souls, her miniature trunk raised in a silent, mournful trumpet, a lament for their lost lives, their stolen futures.

Their encounter with Lulu was a fragile, heartbreaking affair. Her memory was indeed shattered, her thoughts a confusing jumble of fragmented images, of celestial choirs, of battles fought alongside an angel of light, of a terrible betrayal, of a fall into darkness. She spoke of Zariel, not as the Archduchess of Avernus, but as a beloved friend, a valiant commander, a champion of justice. And she spoke, with a child-like innocence that was both touching and terrifying, of a sword, a blade of pure, celestial light, the Sword of Zariel, which she believed held the key to her friend’s redemption, and perhaps, to the salvation of Elturel.

The souls in chains were a constant, terrible reminder of the stakes. But in Lulu’s fragmented memories, in her unwavering, if naive, belief in Zariel’s potential for redemption, Kaelen, Rhys, and Reya found a new, desperate focus for their quest. The Sword of Zariel. If it existed, if it could be found, it might be their only hope of confronting the Archduchess, of breaking Elturel’s pact, of perhaps even turning the tide in this eternal, infernal war.

##### **Chapter 8: The Archdevil's Bargain**

(Master Draft)

Lulu the hollyphant, her mind a shattered mosaic of sorrowful memories and childlike hope, became their unlikely, and often frustrating, guide through the treacherous wastes of Avernus. Her fragmented recollections of Zariel’s past, of a time when the Archduchess was still an angel of light, and of a legendary celestial blade known as the Sword of Zariel, offered the only tangible path towards confronting the ruler of this infernal realm and potentially freeing Elturel from its chains.

Their quest for the Sword led them to some of the most dangerous and iconic locations in Avernus. They navigated the shifting, bone-littered battlefields of the Blood War, their infernal war machine often their only defense against marauding demons and disciplined devil patrols. They sought counsel from strange, enigmatic beings who had carved out precarious existences in this hellscape: a coven of night hags whose knowledge of Avernus’s secrets was as vast as their cruelty; a fallen celestial, his wings tattered, his spirit broken, who offered cryptic clues in exchange for a fleeting moment of remembered grace; and even, in a moment of sheer desperation, they bartered with a pit fiend, one of Zariel’s most powerful lieutenants, its very presence an aura of overwhelming menace, its bargains etched in contracts of soul-binding damnation.

Kaelen, his understanding of infernal politics and his connection to Jergal’s grim wisdom, proved crucial in these encounters. He navigated the treacherous currents of diabolical negotiation, his words carefully chosen, his offers precise, always seeking to gain information, to secure passage, to forge temporary, uneasy alliances, without forfeiting their souls or their ultimate purpose. He learned that the Sword of Zariel was more than just a legend; it was a potent artifact, a symbol of Zariel’s lost grace, hidden away in a place known as the Bleeding Citadel, a fortress that had once been a celestial bastion before its fall and corruption.

Rhys, his hunter’s pragmatism often at odds with the surreal, morally ambiguous choices they were forced to make in Avernus, struggled with the compromises, the deals with devils, the constant proximity to absolute evil. His arrows, though still deadly, felt almost insignificant against the overwhelming scale of infernal power. Yet, his unwavering loyalty to his companions, his determination to protect the innocent souls of Elturel, kept him fighting, kept him searching for a path of righteousness even in this realm of damnation.

Reya Mantlemorn, her initial, all-consuming grief for Elturel slowly transforming into a burning, focused resolve, found a kindred spirit in Lulu. The hollyphant’s unwavering belief in Zariel’s potential for redemption, however naive, resonated with Reya’s own desperate hope that her city, her people, could still be saved. She became Lulu’s protector, her fierce Hellrider’s courage a shield against the horrors of Avernus.

Their journey eventually led them to the court of Bel, the former Archduke of Avernus, a powerful pit fiend who had been supplanted by Zariel and now languished in a state of bitter, resentful exile within his own crumbling fortress. Bel, a creature of immense power and ancient, cunning intelligence, saw in the heroes a potential tool, a means to perhaps reclaim his lost position, or at least, to sow chaos within Zariel’s ranks.

The audience with Bel was a terrifying, high-stakes negotiation. The pit fiend, his form wreathed in shadow and flame, his eyes burning with an infernal light, offered them a bargain: he would provide them with the means to reach the Bleeding Citadel, to confront Zariel, even to potentially wield the Sword against her. In return, he demanded not their souls, but a promise. If they succeeded in defeating Zariel, or in significantly weakening her power, they would support his claim to once again rule Avernus.

It was a devil’s bargain, a choice between two evils, a path fraught with unimaginable peril and moral ambiguity. Kaelen, his mind a cold vortex of calculation, weighed the options. Supporting Bel was anathema, a betrayal of everything they fought for. Yet, without his aid, their quest to reach the Sword, to save Elturel, was likely doomed. Jergal’s whispers were a chilling, pragmatic counsel: in the Nine Hells, sometimes the only path forward was paved with lesser evils.

With a heavy heart, Kaelen accepted Bel’s terms, the pact sealed not with blood, but with a grim, unspoken understanding of the terrible choices one must make in the face of absolute despair. Bel, true to his word, provided them with an infernal map, detailing the treacherous paths to the Bleeding Citadel, and a potent, if volatile, infernal artifact – a Soul Coin, imbued with the captured essence of a powerful, tormented soul – that could power their war machine through Avernus’s most dangerous territories, and perhaps, even breach the Citadel’s formidable defenses.

The Archdevil’s bargain had been struck. Their path to the Bleeding Citadel, to the Sword of Zariel, and to the final, desperate confrontation with the Archduchess of Avernus, was now open. But the price of their passage, the weight of their infernal alliance, was a heavy burden, a chilling reminder that even in victory, some part of their souls might forever be lost to the shadows of the Nine Hells.

### **Book 6: Beyond the Second Sundering: Thread of Gate, Thread of Flame**

#### **Series Title: Chronicles of the Fifth Age: Tales From Beyond the Second Sundering**

#### **Part 3: The Price of Redemption**

##### **Chapter 9: The Bleeding Citadel**

(Master Draft)

The Soul Coin, a pulsating disc of cold, malevolent energy gifted by the pit fiend Bel, thrummed in Kaelen’s grasp, its captured soul whispering silent, tormented pleas. It was a grim currency for passage through Avernus, but it powered their battered Devil’s Ride, propelling them across the blood-soaked plains towards their next desperate objective: the Bleeding Citadel. Lulu, the amnesiac hollyphant, hovered anxiously beside Reya Mantlemorn, her fragmented memories of Zariel and a legendary sword their only true compass in this infernal wasteland.

The journey to the Bleeding Citadel was a descent into deeper circles of Avernian horror. They navigated rivers of boiling blood, their infernal war machine skittering across bridges of bone and sinew. They outran patrols of narzugons, fiendish knights mounted on nightmarish steeds, their hellfire lances leaving trails of spectral flame. They witnessed colossal battles between legions of devils and surging hordes of demons, the very ground trembling with the impact of their unholy war. Rhys’s arrows, though few, were saved for moments of dire need, while Kaelen’s Whisper tasted fiendish blood more than once as they defended their precarious vehicle from opportunistic scavengers and lesser fiends.

The Bleeding Citadel, when they finally beheld it, was a sight of terrible, tragic majesty. It rose from a vast, rust-colored plain, a colossal fortress of white marble, its celestial architecture a stark, defiant contrast to the hellish landscape. Yet, it was deeply wounded. Great, gaping rents scarred its once-gleaming walls, from which oozed a constant, slow stream of what looked like divine blood, staining the plains around it in an ever-widening crimson circle. This had once been a bastion of good, a celestial outpost, before Zariel herself, in her angelic prime, had led a doomed charge into Avernus and fallen. The Citadel, like its former commander, was now a casualty, a bleeding monument to a lost cause.

Lulu, upon seeing the Citadel, let out a mournful cry, a sound that resonated with an ancient, heartbreaking sorrow. Fragments of memory seemed to surface in her clouded sapphire eyes. “The Citadel… our last stand… Zariel… she fought like a lioness… but the darkness… it was too strong…”

The approach to the Citadel was guarded not by devils, but by something far more insidious: despair. The very air around it was thick with the echoes of fallen angels, their whispers of betrayal, of broken oaths, of hope extinguished. Illusions preyed on their minds, showing them visions of their own failures, their deepest fears, seeking to break their spirits before they even reached the Citadel’s gates. Reya, her Hellrider’s faith already battered, struggled against visions of Elturel consumed by fire, its citizens dragged into eternal torment. Rhys saw the blighted Dessarin Valley, the monstrous forms of the elemental prophets, the faces of those he had failed to protect. Kaelen, his connection to Jergal a cold, anchoring presence, saw glimpses of the Raven Queen’s shadowed realm, of the endless, indifferent cycle of souls, and felt the chilling weight of his own, uncertain destiny.

It was Lulu, her innocent, unwavering belief in Zariel’s potential for redemption a fragile shield against the Citadel’s psychic assault, who guided them through the illusions. “The sword… it is within… it remembers the light…” she whispered, her small voice a beacon in the oppressive gloom.
